<OTHER>Department of East Asian Languages and Civilizations</OTHER>

<M10>A Brief History of EALC and Asian Studies at Harvard</M10>

<M02>Chinese language was first taught at Harvard during a span of three years, from 1879 to 1882, by Chinese scholar Ge Kunhua but was not taught on a regular basis until 1921.</M02>
<M02>In 1928, through the generous bequest of Charles Martin Hall the Harvard-Yenching Institute was formed in partnership with China's Yenching University, laying a strong foundation for Harvard's commitment to the study of Asia.</M02>

<M02>Harvard began to offer Japanese language instruction on a regular basis in 1931.</M02>
<M02>In 1937 Chinese and Japanese instruction, previously offered in the Department of Semitic Languages and History, found a more appropriate home in the newly created Department of Far Eastern Languages (also referred to at the time as the Division of Far Eastern Languages.)</M02>
<M02>In 1941, the Department of Far Eastern Languages offered its first Ph.D., jointly with the Department of History.</M02>

<M02>Most Ph.D. degrees awarded in Far Eastern Languages between 1941 and 1972 were in fact joint degrees in History and Far Eastern Languages.</M02>
<M02>In 1939 the undergraduate course "History of East Asian Civilization" was offered for the first time.</M02>

<M02>The course began to be offered yearly in 1946 and included a one semester introduction to Chinese civilization taught by John King Fairbank and one semester on Japan taught by Edwin O.</M02>
<M02>Reischauer.</M02>
<M05>These courses are taught to this day, currently as Societies of the World 12 and 13, China: Tradition and Transformation and Japan in Asia and the World, respectively.</M05>
<M02>In 1972 the department changed its name from Far Eastern Languages to East Asian Languages and Civilizations.</M02>

<M05>In the same year, the Council on East Asian Studies was created to oversee an undergraduate concentration in East Asian Studies.</M05>
<M05>Between 1972 and 1990 undergraduates interested in East Asia could select between concentrations in East Asian Studies, which focused on social science and EALC which concerned itself with the humanities.</M05>
<M02>In 1990 the undergraduate concentrations were joined into one program under the auspices of the Department.</M02>

<M02>Timeline</M02>

<OTHER>1879-82</OTHER>
<M02>Chinese taught at Harvard by Ge Kunhua (Ko K’un-hua 戈鯤化) (1836-1882)</M02>

<OTHER>1913-14</OTHER>
<M05>The first courses in East Asian thought are taught at Harvard in the Department of Philosophy by the Professor of Japanese Literature and Life.</M05>
<M06>This visiting professorship is organized by Professor James Haughton Woods, a specialist in Indian philosophy and a faculty member in the Philosophy Department.</M06>
<M06>The chair is supported by an endowment donated by multiple benefactors, of whom the largest is William Sturgis Bigelow, a famous collector of Japanese art and the source of much of the Boston MFA’s Japanese collection.</M06>

<M10>The first person to hold the position is Professor Masaharu Anesaki, who offers “Philosophy 5: Religious and Moral Developments of the Japanese, with reference to Philosophy, Art and Literature” and “Philosophy 24: Schools of the Religious and Philosophical Thought of Japan, as compared with those of India and China.”</M10>

<OTHER>1914</OTHER>
<M10>Charles Hall, co-founder of Alcoa, dies.</M10>
<M10>His will leaves approximately $6.5 million for the promotion of Chinese studies in China.</M10>

<OTHER>1914-15</OTHER>
<M03>Professor Masaharu Anesaki offers the first courses at Harvard dedicated to the topics of Japanese Buddhism and literature.</M03>
<M07>He teaches “Philosophy 11a: Buddhist Ethics and Japanese Life” in the fall and “Philosophy 11b: Religion and Poetry in Japan” in the spring.</M07>
<M07>In addition, Anesaki repeats his year-long course “Philosophy 5: Religious and Moral Development of the Japanese.”</M07>

<OTHER>1915</OTHER>
<M02>Hall Estate trust established.</M02>

<OTHER>1915-16</OTHER>
<M03>First courses dedicated to the subject of Confucian thought are taught at Harvard by Professor Hattori of the Imperial University of Tokyo, who replaces Anesaki as Professor of Japanese Literature and Life.</M03>
<M03>Hattori offers three half-courses: “Philosophy 5a: Confucian Ethics and Japanese Life,” “Philosophy 11c: Confucius, his Life and Teachings,” and ”Philosophy 11d: Schools of Confucian Thought in Japan.”</M03>
<M10>Hattori’s salary depletes the majority of the remaining funds in the endowment, and the position is not renewed.</M10>

<OTHER>1916</OTHER>
<M10>Professor Woods, at the time chair of the Department of Philosophy, formally reestablishes the teaching of Chinese at Harvard by creating a Chair in Chinese.</M10>
<M10>The existence of this Chair is later used to persuade the trustees of the Hall estate that Harvard has an interest in the Far East and will be an adequate shepherd for their funds.</M10>

<M02>1916-17, Spring</M02>
<M02>Early class on Chinese religion taught at Harvard in the Divinity School.</M02>
<M02>The course is offered by Reverends Logan H. Roots and James Thayer Addison, and entitled “Christian Missions in China in Relation to the Chinese Religions.”</M02>

<OTHER>1921-22</OTHER>
<M02>Zhao Yuanren is appointed to a one year renewable position at Harvard as Instructor of Chinese.</M02>
<M02>In the spring of 1922, Zhao offers the first class in Chinese to be taught at Harvard since the death of Ge Kunhua.</M02>
<M02>The half-year course is entitled “Chinese 1: Introduction to the Chinese Language.”</M02>

<OTHER>1922-23</OTHER>
<M02>Zhao’s introductory course is expanded into a year-long class.</M02>

<OTHER>1923-24</OTHER>
<M02>The Chinese curriculum is expanded into two year-long courses (one introductory, one advanced), which are both taught by Zhao.</M02>

<OTHER>1924</OTHER>
<M02>Wallace Brett Donham, Dean of the Graduate School of Business Administration and founder of the case study method, establishes relations between the trustees of the Hall estate and Harvard University.</M02>
<M02>Donham later becomes a long-standing member of the HYI Board of Trustees.</M02>

<OTHER>1924-25</OTHER>
<M02>Mei Guangdi 梅光迪 replaces Zhao Yuanren as Instructor of Chinese.</M02>

<OTHER>1925-26</OTHER>
<M05>In addition to the introductory and advanced Chinese language courses, Mei adds a class on Chinese thought and literature to the curriculum.</M05>
<M05>The course is entitled “Chinese 3: Introduction to the Literature and Philosophy of China.”</M05>

<OTHER>1927-28</OTHER>
<M05>Mei Guangdi further expands the Chinese curriculum, offering a total of five year-long courses.</M05>
<M05>He expands the Chinese language program into three years, continues to offer his literature and philosophy course, and adds a new “Special Studies” course.</M05>
<M05>The special studies class, entitled “Chinese 20,” continues in subsequent years, and appears to be the primary venue through which graduate students are trained.</M05>

<M02>1928, January 4</M02>
<M02>Harvard-Yenching Institute formally incorporated.</M02>
<M02>First meeting of Trustees.</M02>

<M02>1928, April 25</M02>
<M02>HYI Trustees approve funding for first visiting professorships, appointing Paul Pelliot, Lucius C.</M02>
<M02>Porter, Baron Alexander von Staël-Holstein, and William Hung for the 1928-29 academic year.</M02>
<M05>These scholars all offer classes in 1928-29 which, in addition to Mei’s ongoing three year course in the Chinese language, dramatically expand the number of courses available on East Asian subjects.</M05>

<M01>1928-29, Fall
Professor Paul Pelliot, visiting for one semester from the College de France, teaches “Chinese 10: The Chief Periods in the History of Chinese Art.” 23 students enroll, more than the total enrollment in all other Chinese courses combined.</M01>

<M02>1928-29, Spring</M02>
<M02>First class in Tibetan taught at Harvard.</M02>
<M02>The course is offered by Professor Baron Alexander von Staël-Holstein of Yenching University and entitled “Chinese 23: Introduction to the Tibetan Language.”</M02>
<M07>In addition, von Staël-Holstein also teaches “Chinese 21: Introduction to the Kacyapaparivarta.”</M07>

<M05>First survey course in Chinese history taught at Harvard by Professor William Hung (Hong Ye) of Yenching University.</M05>
<M04>The half-course is titled “Chinese 24: Introduction to the Study of Chinese history.”</M04>
<M06>3 graduate students enroll.</M06>

<M01>1928-29
Professor Lucius Porter of Yenching University teaches a year-long survey course entitled “Chinese 12: Survey of Chinese Thought.”</M01>

<M05>Enrollment in Mei’s Chinese language classes nearly double from the previous year, increasing to a total of 9 students.</M05>

<M04>First Harvard University documentation of graduate students whose area of study lay chiefly in Chinese.</M04>
<M04>Five graduate students focus on Chinese studies.</M04>
<M02>While this number increases to as many as ten Harvard students at a time during the years 1928-1936, attrition is evidently quite high, as only a total of three advanced degrees (2 A.M. / 1 Ph.D.) are awarded.</M02>

<OTHER>1929</OTHER>
<M02>HYI is assigned five rooms in basement of Boylston Hall.</M02>

<M02>First A.M. degree granted in Chinese.</M02>

<M02>Wing Tsit Chan is awarded the first Ph.D. in an East Asian subject at Harvard for a dissertation presented to the Department of Philosophy entitled “The Philosophy of Chuang Tzu.”</M02>

<OTHER>1929-30</OTHER>
<M05>After the surge of the previous year, course offerings and student enrollments decline.</M05>
<M05>A total of four classes are offered.</M05>
<M05>Mei teaches only two Chinese classes (beginning and intermediate).</M05>
<M07>William Hung remains at Harvard for a second year and teaches “Chinese 11: History of Chinese Civilization.”</M07>
<M04>Together, he and Mei also offer a graduate research class.</M04>

<M01>1930-31, Spring
Professor DeVargas visits from Yenching University and teaches one half-course: “Chinese 12: The Cultural Renaissance in China.”</M01>

<M02>1931, Oct.</M02>
<OTHER>30</OTHER>
<M02>HYI Trustees agree to invite Professor Serge Elisséeff to Harvard for visiting lectureships in the year 1932-33.</M02>

<OTHER>1931-32</OTHER>
<M02>First course in the Japanese language taught at Harvard.</M02>
<M02>The year-long class is taught by Hideo Kishimoto and entitled “Japanese 1: Elementary Japanese.”</M02>
<M02>Five students – three undergraduates and two graduates – enroll.</M02>
<M04>In the same year, Kishimoto inaugurates a graduate research class to match the one offered on the China side.</M04>
<M04>It is entitled “Japanese 20: Reading and Research.”</M04>

<M02>1931-32, Fall</M02>
<M02>Professor Lucius Porter of Yenching University returns to Harvard as a visiting professor on behalf of the Harvard-Yenching Institute.</M02>
<M07>He teaches two half-courses, an undergraduate class “Chinese 12: Survey of Chinese Thought” and a graduate course “Chinese 22: Mencius on Human Nature and Political Philosophy.”</M07>

<M07>James Ware, a graduate student in Chinese, co-teaches Chinese 1 with Mei.</M07>

<M07>Early 1930s</M07>
<M04>Substantial cutbacks in HYI funding for Asian studies at Harvard and in China, resulting from reduced dividends on endowment investments.</M04>

<M05>Graduate students supported by HYI and affiliated with Harvard during this period include John Fairbank and Edwin Reischauer.</M05>

<OTHER>1932</OTHER>
<M05>James Ware receives the first Ph.D. degree in Chinese studies to be granted by Harvard.</M05>
<M05>A specialist in Pre-Tang Chinese Buddhism, his dissertation is entitled “Wei Shou on Chinese Buddhism.”</M05>
<M02>A portion of this dissertation is published in T’oung-pao 29 (1933).</M02>

<M02>1932, July 6 - Aug.</M02>
<OTHER>16</OTHER>
<M05>First Summer Seminar in Far Eastern Studies held at Harvard University.</M05>

<OTHER>1932-33</OTHER>
<M01>James Ware, having received his doctorate, fills in for Mei (who is on leave) as Instructor of Chinese.</M01>
<M05>In addition to Chinese 1 and 2, he teaches a half-course in the spring entitled “Chinese 10: The Historical and Intellectual Background of Chinese Literature.”</M05>

<M05>Serge Elisséeff teaches at Harvard for the first time.</M05>
<M07>He teaches two classes – “Chinese 10: Explanation of a Chinese Historical Text” which focuses on Ssu-ma Ch’ien, and “Japanese 2: History of Japanese Civilization before 1800.”</M07>

<OTHER>1933</OTHER>
<M07>HYI receives gift of the two stone lions that presently stand on the lawn in front of 2 Divinity Avenue.</M07>

<M02>1933, Nov.</M02>
<OTHER>13</OTHER>
<M07>HYI Trustees appoint Elisséeff Director of the Harvard-Yenching Institute on a lifetime basis and nominate him Professor of Far Eastern Languages of Harvard University, effective September 1, 1934.</M07>

<OTHER>1933-34</OTHER>
<M07>Elisséeff is sent to China by the HYI to learn colloquial Chinese and consult with the Yenching side of HYI’s operations.</M07>
<M07>Kishimoto remains as the sole Japanese instructor.</M07>
<M05>In addition to language classes, he teaches two half-courses, one each semester: “Japanese 10a: The Historical Background of Japanese Civilization” and “Japanese 10b: History of Japanese Religions.”</M05>

<M05>Mei returns and co-teaches Chinese language classes with James Ware.</M05>
<M05>They are joined by Charles Gardner, a graduate student in the History Department, who teaches “Chinese 20: The History of China.”</M05>
<M02>This is the first course offered in the Chinese curriculum to be cross-listed with the History department, as “History 20L.”</M02>

<OTHER>1934-35</OTHER>
<M02>Elisséeff returns from China to assume day-to-day directorship of the Harvard-Yenching Institute.</M02>
<M02>He also resumes teaching classes on Japanese language, early Japanese history, and Chinese classics.</M02>
<OTHER>Kishimoto leaves.</OTHER>

<M05>Graduate course offerings in Chinese are expanded to encompass more methodological and theoretical themes.</M05>
<M07>Mei teaches “Chinese 5: Chinese Theories of Literature and History,” while Gardner teaches “Chinese 12: Historical Method in the Study of Chinese History.”</M07>
<M07>A total of three students enroll in the two courses, one in the former and two in the latter.</M07>

<M07>This is Mei Guangdi’s final year at Harvard.</M07>

<OTHER>1935</OTHER>
<M07>Harvard-Yenching Institute Monograph Series initiated.</M07>
<M07>The first title is Albert Herrmann, Historical and Commercial Atlas of China.</M07>
<M07>It is published by Harvard University Press.</M07>
<M07>Charles Sidney Gardner receives his Ph.D. in history with a dissertation entitled “A Chapter from the Basic Annals of the Draft Tsing History.”</M07>
<M07>He assumes a position as Instructor in Chinese.</M07>

<OTHER>1935-36</OTHER>
<M07>Teaching staff in Chinese and Japanese is limited to James Ware, Charles Gardner, and Serge Elisséeff.</M07>
<M05>Ware continues to offer the three-year course in Chinese.</M05>
<M05>Elisséeff expands the Japanese language program into a two-year course (elementary and intermediate).</M05>
<M05>Charles Gardner offers a new year-long survey of Chinese history entitled “Chinese 11: History of China.</M05>
<M05>Evolution of Chinese Culture from Antiquity to the Present.”</M05>

<M05>There are only three Harvard graduate students in Chinese (none in Japanese) at this time.</M05>

<OTHER>1936</OTHER>
<M07>Inaugural issue of the Harvard Journal of Asiatic Studies published.</M07>

<OTHER>1936-37</OTHER>
<M02>John Fairbank joins the faculty of the History department and teaches his first course at Harvard.</M02>
<M02>It is titled “History 83b: History of the Far East since 1793.”</M02>
<M06>The course is immediately popular, attracting a total of 43 students.</M06>

<M05>Gardner’s survey of Chinese history is relisted as “History 85: History of China – Political, Institutional and Cultural Evolution from Antiquity to the Present” and cross-listed with the Chinese language program.</M05>
<M05>Elisséeff’s Japanese history course is also cross-listed with the History department for the first time.</M05>

<M05>Three teachers, one Japanese and two Chinese (Mr. Wang, Weng, and Dr. Shimoyama), join the language teaching staff as assistant instructors.</M05>

<M05>In anticipation of the formation of FEL, the number of graduate students in Far Eastern Languages increase to 12, a 300% increase over the previous year.</M05>

<M02>1937, Feb.</M02>
<OTHER>9</OTHER>
<M02>Creation of the Division of Far Eastern Languages</M02>

<M02>Division of Far Eastern Languages formally founded by vote of the Harvard Faculty.</M02>
<M02>Elisséeff appointed first chair.</M02>
<M02>FEL as a “Department” can be found in the minutes of faculty meetings, and the term “Division” ceases to appear after the late 1930s.</M02>

<OTHER>1937</OTHER>
<M02>First A.M. degree granted in Far Eastern Languages.</M02>

<OTHER>1937-38</OTHER>
<M02>Elisséeff offers a new year-long, mixed level course entitled “Japanese 15: History of Japanese Literature.”</M02>
<M02>This is the first course dedicated to subject of Japanese literature to be taught at Harvard.</M02>

<M02>First undergraduate concentrator in Far Eastern Languages.</M02>

<M02>1937, October 5</M02>
<M10>Appointment of Elisséeff as first Chairman of the Division of Far Eastern Languages is announced at the Harvard faculty meeting.</M10>

<M02>1938, March 22</M02>
<M02>Faculty votes to change primary administrative subdivision of the Faculty from Division to Department.</M02>
<M07>“Two or more Departments may, at their discretion, organize themselves into a Division and delegate to that Division such authority as they may desire.”</M07>
<M07>This change takes effect as of the 1939-40 academic year.</M07>
<M07>Hereafter FEL is known exclusively as a Department.</M07>

<OTHER>1938</OTHER>
<M02>First Ph.D. in Far Eastern Studies awarded to Tu-chien Weng, a specialist in Mongol history, for a dissertation entitled “The Life of Ai-hsieh.”</M02>
<M02>Weng received his B.A. and M.A. from Yenching University, and is evidence that the close relationship between Harvard and Yenching fostered by the Harvard-Yenching Institute affected not just faculty appointments, but also graduate student admissions.</M02>
<M06>After receiving his degree, Weng assumes a traveling fellowship in Paris under the auspices of the Harvard-Yenching Institute.</M06>

<OTHER>1938-39</OTHER>
<M06>Japanese and Chinese are grouped together for the first time in the Reports of the President of Harvard College and the Harvard Course Catalogue under the rubric “Far Eastern Languages.”</M06>

<M06>Elisséeff adds a third, advanced year to the Japanese language curriculum.</M06>

<M04>In his final graduate year, Edwin O.</M04>
<M04>Reischauer joins the FEL faculty as an instructor.</M04>
<OTHER>He teaches beginning and intermediate Chinese.</OTHER>

<M05>Undergraduate concentrators in Far Eastern Languages increase to a total of three students.</M05>

<OTHER>1939</OTHER>
<M05>Reischauer receives his Ph.D. in Chinese Studies from Harvard with a special field in Japanese history.</M05>
<M05>His dissertation is titled "Ennin's Diary of his Travels in T'ang China, 838-847."</M05>
<M04>He assumes the post of Instructor in Far Eastern Languages.</M04>

<OTHER>1939-40</OTHER>
<M04>Fairbank and Reischauer inaugurate what will come to be popularly known as the “Rice Paddies” course.</M04>
<M05>In their first years, the two semesters of the course are taught as “Chinese 10a: Survey of the History of Eastern Asia from Early Times to 1500” and “History 83b: Survey of the History of Eastern Asia from 1500 to the Present Time.”</M05>
<M02>The first semester is co-taught by Reischauer and Fairbank.</M02>
<M02>The second semester is taught by Fairbank alone.</M02>
<M02>In latter parlance, the class is most typically referred to as the “History of East Asian Civilization.”</M02>

<M08>Fairbank initiates Harvard’s broader academic community outreach efforts in the field of East Asian studies by organizing the Far Eastern Institute, which is sponsored by the Committees on Chinese and Japanese Studies of the American Council of Learned Societies, and further supported by Harvard-Yenching Institute.</M08>
<M08>The primary goal of the Institute is to “meet the needs of college and secondary-school teachers who desired a fundamental background in the cultures of the Far East” by offering instruction in the history and art of China and Japan.</M08>

<OTHER>1940</OTHER>
<M08>Harvard’s third and fourth Ph.D. degrees in Far Eastern Studies are awarded to Yen Yi Huang and Yueh-hwa Lin.</M08>
<M08>Huang and Lin are graduates of Lingnan and Yenching universities, respectively, both of which were supported by the Harvard-Yenching Institute.</M08>

<OTHER>1940-41</OTHER>
<M02>The Rice Paddies course is renamed “Chinese 10a: Cultural History of the Far East,” and “History 83b: The Modern Far East.”</M02>
<M02>In a pattern that would henceforth become standard, Reischauer teaches the first term, John King Fairbank the second.</M02>

<M02>1941, May 20</M02>
<M02>Faculty of Arts and Sciences votes to approve the establishment of a degree of Ph.D. in History and Far Eastern Languages, to be administered by a special committee appointed by the President.</M02>
<M02>Fairbank is the principal faculty force behind the establishment of the new program.</M02>

<OTHER>1941-42</OTHER>
<M02>Francis Cleaves, in his final year of graduate studies, joins the faculty of FEL and assumes responsibility for teaching the first year Chinese course.</M02>

<M02>1942, February</M02>
<M02>Following the outbreak of WWII, Elisséeff and Reischauer initiate a special spring term introductory Japanese class that is vastly oversubscribed.</M02>
<M02>Total enrollment in Elementary Japanese surges from six students before Pearl Harbor to fifty-two in the spring term.</M02>
<M05>Some of these students are later recruited for the special Japanese school run by Reischauer in Washington to train cryptanalysts for the Army Signal Corps.</M05>
<M04>They include Howard Hibbett and Benjamin Schwartz.</M04>

<OTHER>1942</OTHER>
<M04>Francis Cleaves receives his Ph.D. in Far Eastern Studies from Harvard with a special field in Chinese Language of the Mongol Period.</M04>
<M04>His dissertation is titled "A Sino-Mongolian Inscription of 1362."</M04>
<M04>He assumes the post of Faculty Instructor in Far Eastern Languages.</M04>

<OTHER>1942-43</OTHER>
<M04>Professor Zhao Yuanren returns to Harvard to teach an intensive Chinese course similar to that offered on the Japanese side.</M04>
<M04>Intensive elementary Chinese is offered both terms; intensive intermediate Chinese is offered from the spring term.</M04>
<M02>In addition, Zhao also teaches Harvard’s first class on non-Mandarin Chinese dialects, entitled “Chinese 9a: Chinese Dialectology.”</M02>
<M02>Enrollment in Mandarin classes also increases, though not as dramatically as Japanese.</M02>
<M06>A total of 22 students enroll in Elementary Chinese, as opposed to thirteen in the preceding year.</M06>

<M06>Elisséeff and Reischauer continue to offer their intensive course, with Intensive Elementary and Intensive Intermediate Japanese simultaneously offered in both fall and spring terms.</M06>

<OTHER>1946</OTHER>
<M05>Fairbank initiates a full-time seminar leading to an M.A. known as Regional Studies: China.</M05>
<M05>This is later expanded to include the rest of East Asia.</M05>

<M05>James Robert Hightower receives his Ph.D. in Far Eastern Languages with a dissertation titled "The Han shih wai chuan."</M05>
<M04>He is appointed to a position as Instructor of Chinese, but spends the next two years on leave in Peking.</M04>

<M04>Yang Lien-sheng receives his Ph.D. in History and Far Eastern Languages with a dissertation titled "Notes on the Economic History of the Chin Dynasty."</M04>

<OTHER>1947</OTHER>
<M02>Elizabeth Huff receives the first Ph.D. in Far Eastern Languages to be awarded to a Radcliffe student, for a dissertation on Chinese poetics entitled “Shih-hsueh.”</M02>
<M02>Huff began her graduate career at Harvard / Radcliffe in the mid-1930s, and was studying in China at the outbreak of WWII, whereupon she was interned by the Japanese in Shandong for the duration of the war.</M02>
<M02>After graduating from Harvard, she took a position at UC Berkeley, where she established the East Asian Library.</M02>

<OTHER>1947-48</OTHER>
<M02>Mongolian is first offered in FEL as a year-long course taught by Francis Cleaves.</M02>

<M02>Yang Lien-sheng joins the faculty of the Department of Far Eastern Languages as an assistant professor.</M02>
<M05>He initially teaches Chinese language classes and topics in pre-modern Chinese history.</M05>

<M05>After a hiatus of several years, the full two semester Rice Paddies course is reorganized and offered again as part of Harvard’s Gen Ed curriculum under the title “History of Far Eastern Civilization.”</M05>
<M06>119 students enroll in the first semester, 58 in the second.</M06>

<OTHER>1948-49</OTHER>
<M06>James Hightower returns to Harvard as Assistant Professor in Far Eastern Languages.</M06>
<M06>He teaches Elementary Chinese, Intermediate Chinese, and the History of Chinese Literature.</M06>

<OTHER>1950</OTHER>
<M06>Harvard-Yenching Institute Studies Series initiated.</M06>
<M06>The first title is An Annotated Bibliography of Selected Chinese Reference Works, compiled by Ssŭ-yü Teng and Knight Biggerstaff.</M06>

<M04>Howard Scott Hibbett and John Whitney Hall both receive Ph.D. degrees in Far Eastern Languages.</M04>
<M04>Hibbett’s dissertation is titled “Ejima Kiseki and the Hachimonjiya: a Study in Eighteenth-century Japanese Fiction.”</M04>
<M10>Hall’s is titled “Modern Trends in Tokugawa Japan: the Life and Policies of Tanuma Okitsugu.”</M10>
<M10>Hibbett spends a year as a member of the Society of Fellows, and then assumes a teaching position at UCLA.</M10>
<M02>Hall goes on to become a founding figure in the study of premodern Japanese history in the United States, teaching first at Michigan and then at Yale.</M02>

<M02>Benjamin Isadore Schwartz receives his Ph.D. in History and Far Eastern Languages, with a dissertation entitled “Communism in China until the Rise of Mao Tse-tung.”</M02>
<M07>He is appointed Assistant Professor of History and Government.</M07>

<OTHER>1952</OTHER>
<M05>FEL inaugurates its Korean program, which includes an intensive course in the Korean language open only to graduate students, and a general course in Korean civilization available both to undergraduates and graduates.</M05>
<M05>In its early years, the program is overseen by Professor Doo Soo Suh, former Dean of Seoul National University, who teaches all classes.</M05>
<M05>Suh’s position as Visiting Lecturer is renewed annually.</M05>

<OTHER>1954</OTHER>
<M05>Elisséeff’s annual report on the state of the department identifies an FEL teaching staff of 8.</M05>
<M05>Two full professors (Elisséeff and Reischauer); four associate professors (Cleaves, Hightower, Ware, Yang); two visiting lecturers (Ch’en and Doo Soo Suh)</M05>

<OTHER>1954-55</OTHER>
<M05>Hightower inaugurates a second course as part of Harvard’s Gen Ed curriculum.</M05>
<M07>Entitled “Humanities 112: Classics of the Far East,” the course has 24 enrollees in its first year.</M07>

<OTHER>1956</OTHER>
<M07>Elisséeff retires.</M07>
<M10>Reischauer assumes directorship of HYI and serves as Chair of FEL until his appointment as ambassador to Japan in 1961.</M10>

<M02>1958, September</M02>
<M02>FEL and HYI relocate from Boylston Hall to 2 Divinity Avenue.</M02>

<OTHER>1958</OTHER>
<M02>Howard Hibbett returns to Harvard as Elisséeff’s replacement.</M02>
<M07>He initially holds the rank of Associate Professor of Japanese.</M07>

<M07>Edward Wagner joins the faculty of FEL, initially as Lecturer in Korean.</M07>
<M02>He reinitiates the Korean language program, which had been in hiatus since the departure of Doo Soo Suh in 1955.</M02>
<M06>When promoted to assistant professor the following year, his becomes the first permanent faculty position in Korean.</M06>

<M07>Nagatomi Masatoshi joins the faculty of FEL as its first fulltime professor of Buddhist studies.</M07>
<M10>While Kenneth Ch’en had taught courses in Buddhism in previous years as a visiting lecturer, Nagatomi’s appointment makes the position permanent.</M10>

<OTHER>1950-60</OTHER>
<M10>Over the course of this decade, the number of undergraduate concentrators in FEL increase significantly.</M10>
<M02>In 1950, three Harvard undergraduates concentrated in FEL.</M02>
<M02>By 1961, that number had increased to twenty-two.</M02>
<M05>Nevertheless, as a concentration FEL remained quite small.</M05>
<M02>The number of concentrators in 1961 constituted one half of one percent of the Harvard’s undergraduate population.</M02>

<OTHER>1960</OTHER>
<M02>Itasaka Gen joins the faculty as Lecturer in Japanese.</M02>
<M10>He and Hibbett assume stewardship of the Japanese program during Reischauer’s appointment as US ambassador to Japan (1961-66).</M10>

<OTHER>1961-65</OTHER>
<M01>James Hightower serves as Chair of FEL.</M01>

<OTHER>1964-66</OTHER>
<M05>Enrollment in East Asian related courses (Area Studies, not language) doubles.</M05>
<M06>These numbers persist through the Vietnam years, with enrollment in the Rice Paddies course regularly exceeding 500 students.</M06>
<M05>In 1966, Harvard has the greatest enrollment in East Asian courses of any major university in America.</M05>
<M05>During this period the Rice Paddies course is reorganized into separate semesters on China and Japan, a division that remains today.</M05>

<OTHER>1965-70</OTHER>
<M01>Howard Hibbett serves as Chair of FEL.</M01>

<OTHER>1966</OTHER>
<M02>Joseph Fletcher is appointed Assistant Professor in the Department of Far Eastern Languages and teaches Harvard’s first course on the Manchu language.</M02>

<OTHER>1970</OTHER>
<M02>The faculty of FEL include 16 tenured or tenure-track professors and 11 lecturers.</M02>
<M02>This represents an expansion of over one hundred per cent since the mid-1950s.</M02>

<OTHER>1960s – 1980s</OTHER>
<M06>Harvard University gradually takes over responsibility for funding the Department of FEL / EALC and the Harvard-Yenching Library from the Harvard-Yenching Institute.</M06>
<M06>As late as the mid-1980s, some positions continue to be funded by HYI, and HYI continues to provide substantial support for book purchases by the library to the present day.</M06>

<OTHER>1967</OTHER>
<M06>Fundraising begins to support a Chair in Vietnamese Studies.</M06>

<OTHER>1970-74</OTHER>
<M01>Edward Wagner serves as Chair of FEL / EALC.</M01>

<OTHER>1971-72</OTHER>
<M02>Vietnamese language first taught at Harvard by Alexander Woodside, the first holder of the newly formed Kenneth T.</M02>
<M02>Young Professorship of Sino-Vietnamese History.</M02>
<M02>Woodside’s primary affiliation is with the History department, but his is cross-listed in FEL / EALC.</M02>

<OTHER>1971-80</OTHER>
<M07>Substantial decline in this period in the amount of graduate student funding supplied by FLAS, NDLF, and Fulbright.</M07>
<M07>The Council on East Asian Studies is created to address the problem of fundraising.</M07>

<M02>1972, Feb.</M02>
<OTHER>22</OTHER>
<M02>FEL renamed EALC</M02>

<M02>Permanent members of the faculty of FEL vote unanimously to change the name of the department from Far Eastern Languages to East Asian Languages and Civilizations.</M02>

<M02>1972, April 11</M02>
<M04>Significant changes to the organization and nomenclature of East Asian fields of study authorized by vote of the Faculty of Arts and Sciences.</M04>
<M04>Council on East Asian Studies, with John K.</M04>
<M02>Fairbank as first chair, created to supervise interdepartmental committees concerned with East Asian Studies.</M02>
<M05>Undergraduate concentration in East Asian Studies created and overseen by an inter-departmental committee chaired by Ezra Vogel.</M05>
<M02>Ph.D. in History and Far Eastern Languages renamed History and East Asian Languages.</M02>

<OTHER>1974-77</OTHER>
<M01>Patrick Hanan serves as Chair of EALC.</M01>

<OTHER>1976</OTHER>
<M06>The Harvard-Yenching Library is placed under the direct administration of Harvard University and integrated into the University library system.</M06>
<M06>Its rare books collection remains the property of HYI.</M06>

<OTHER>2 Divinity Avenue</OTHER>

<M06>Visitors to the Department of East Asian Languages and Civilizations, and the neighboring Harvard-Yenching Institute and Library, which share the same building, often remark on the contrast between the neo-Georgian architectural style of the brick and marble edifice at 2 Divinity and the institutions housed therein.</M06>
<M06>In particular, the marble reliefs that adorn the building’s outer walls carry almost nothing that signifies East Asia, and indeed the marble relief map over the main door shows only the Western hemisphere (fig. 1).</M06>
<M02>While the stone lions, a gift to the Harvard-Yenching Institute by the Starr family in 1962, help to mark the building as a center for Asian studies, the contrast remains.</M02>

<M02>This serves as a reminder that the building was not originally designed to house a department for East Asian studies.</M02>
<M02>The main structure was built during the years 1930 and 1931 as the home for a new Institute of Geographical Exploration.</M02>
<M02>This initial purpose explains the distinctive reliefs on the building’s façade.</M02>

<M06>The building was commissioned by Alexander H. Rice with the financial support of his wife, Eleanor Elkins Widener Rice, who came from one of the wealthiest families in Philadelphia.</M06>
<M02>She had previously funded the construction of Widener Library in honor of her son, Harry Elkins Widener, who perished along with his father, Eleanor’s first husband, George Widener, when the Titanic sank in 1912.</M02>
<M02>Widener stipulated that the architect for the new library be Horace Trumbauer & Associates (fig. 2), the firm behind several buildings and lavish homes built for the Elkins and Widener families, including the Newport mansion Miramar, in progress at the time of her husband’s death and completed in 1915.</M02>

<M02>The choice of Trumbauer & Associates in 1930 for the Institute of Geographical Exploration at 2 Divinity Avenue is therefore no surprise.</M02>
<M02>The building accords with the symmetrical proportions and neoclassical touches characteristic of other work by the firm.</M02>
<M02>The marble interior, grand staircase, and wrought iron ornamental details evoke the opulence of a residence like Miramar (fig. 3) and demonstrate the kind of extreme wealth that kept the architectural firm in business during the most devastating years of the Great Depression.</M02>
<M02>Although Trumbauer & Associate’s buildings were long attributed to Horace Trumbauer, credit should have gone to the firm’s chief designer Julian Abele (1881–1950) (fig. 4) who designed over one hundred of them, including Widener Library and the building at 2 Divinity Avenue.</M02>
<M02>Acclaimed as the last and best American-based beaux arts architect of the early twentieth century, Abele was also the first African American to graduate with a B.S. degree in architecture in the U.S., which he did from the University of Pennsylvania School of Architecture in 1902.</M02>
<M10>Abele’s work long went unacknowledged, a result of both explicit and implicit racial discrimination, as well as a Trumbauer office policy that forbade staff from signing any drawings leaving the office.</M10>
<M10>Numerous institutions have recently begun to redress this neglect, starting with Duke University, whose West Campus includes thirty buildings designed by Abele between 1924 and 1950.</M10>
<M06>In 2020 Widener Library mounted an exhibition on Julian Abele in its rotunda, among the first efforts to commemorate the architect’s contribution to the College’s most central building.</M06>
<M06>Although the comparatively modest structure at 2 Divinity Avenue has not garnered much attention, it has a role to play in telling the history of Abele’s life and work.</M06>

<M07>Abele’s elegantly proportioned two-story building at 2 Divinity (fig. 5) owes much of its appeal to the shape and placement of the marble details that accentuate the redbrick façade.</M07>
<M07>Horizontal bands of white stone run the width of the structure above and below the windows and beneath the cornice and dentil molding, articulating the floors of the structure and emphasizing its horizontality.</M07>
<M07>To carve the intriguing cartographic and zodiacal images on the stone, Trumbauer & Associates turned to their long-standing collaborators, the stonemasons of John Donnelly, Inc. of New York.</M07>
<M02>The artisans who worked for John Donnelly Sr. (1867–1947) were mostly Irish craftsman in the United States on a temporary basis.</M02>
<M02>John Donnelly, Inc. was responsible for sculptural carvings on several government buildings in Washington D.C., from the Library of Congress and the Capitol to the bronze doors of the Supreme Court Building, whose bas-reliefs were designed by architect Cass Gilbert (1859–1934) and John Donnelly Sr., and carved by John Donnelly Jr. (1903–1970).</M02>
<M02>While 2 Divinity was nearing completion, the firm began working with Trumbauer & Associates on the iconic Duke University Chapel designed by Julian Abele.</M02>
<M02>The idiosyncratic iconographic program on the Duke Chapel seems to derive from a combination of the carvers’ own knowledge as well as input from specific individuals.</M02>
<M04>Likewise, the cartographic and astronomical imagery on the exterior of 2 Divinity almost certainly reflects the wishes of the building’s client Alexander Rice and his ideas about geographical exploration.</M04>

<M02>After the Harvard administration under President Conant eliminated the Geography Department in 1948 and Rice retired as the Geographical Institute’s first and only director in 1951, the Institute shut down and the building at 2 Divinity reverted to direct university oversight.</M02>
<M06>By the 1950s, the expansion in the faculty and course offerings of the Department of Far Eastern Languages, together with the rapidly expanding library holdings in East Asian languages, exerted a significant strain on its then home in Boylston Hall.</M06>
<M06>Given the new availability of 2 Divinity, it was decided that this would make a good home for the Department.</M06>
<M06>Initially, Far Eastern Languages and the Harvard-Yenching Institute shared the building with the Departments of Mathematics and Statistics.</M06>
<M02>In 1957, the building was remodeled and the expansion, which presently houses the Harvard-Yenching Library, was constructed with funds from the Harvard-Yenching Institute.</M02>
<M02>A new lecture hall called the Yenching Auditorium was created by the architectural firm of Shepley, Bulfinch, Richardson and Abbott with an addition to the rear of the building that carefully matched the style and materials of the original structure.</M02>
<M06>Far Eastern Languages and the Harvard-Yenching Institute shared the building with the Department of Statistics until the space it occupied on the fourth floor of the library annex was urgently needed for the growing collection.</M06>
<M02>From that point forward, the only other organization to share the building was the Department of Sanskrit and Indian Studies, now the Department of South Asian Studies, which remained at 2 Divinity until its relocation to 1 Bow Street in 2003.</M02>

<M01>Melissa McCormick</M01>
<M02>Published March 6, 2023</M02>

<M03>Department of East Asian Languages and Civilizations (EALC) Diversity, Equity, and Inclusion</M03>

<M03>The Department of East Asian Languages and Civilizations (EALC) upholds the right of all our members – including faculty, staff, and students – to learn and work to their fullest potential.</M03>
<M03>As our community members hail from various backgrounds, we are committed to actively creating and sustaining a diverse, equitable, and inclusive environment where everyone can feel they belong.</M03>
<M10>EALC adheres to Harvard University’s non-discrimination policies for students and employees.</M10>
<M10>We seek to create a department culture that does not impose obstacles and burdens on its members related to their gender, race, ethnicity, nationality, immigration status, sexual orientation, religion, physical/mental ability, or socio-economic status.</M10>

<M04>Everyone in our community should act in a manner consistent with, and in support of, an environment where we can learn, teach, and research together, as well as interact socially and professionally free of discrimination and harassment.</M04>
<M04>The department subscribes to the need to work for a deeper understanding across our differences and against the violence of racism and its nefarious effects, with a special though not exclusive responsibility regarding anti-Black and anti-Asian/Asian American racism.</M04>

<M04>The EALC community aspires to the following principles and practices.</M04>
<M04>All our members have a role to play in upholding them:</M04>

<M04>- Building a welcoming, safe, diverse, inclusive, supportive, and productive environment.
- Engaging each other with collegiality, equity, and fairness.
- Promoting academic excellence, integrity, and honesty.
- Fostering open academic debate that is premised on mutual respect.
- Encouraging the pursuit of rigorous, scholarly explorations of the East Asian region in all its richness and complexity and in a global context.</M04>

<M10>Edwin O.</M10>
<M10>Reischauer Institute of Japanese Studies</M10>

<OTHER>The Edwin O.</OTHER>
<M04>Reischauer Institute of Japanese Studies (RIJS) at Harvard University is one of the oldest centers in the world supporting research activities concerning Japan and Japanese Studies.</M04>
<M04>Its mission is to advance teaching and research on Japan, to promote a wide-ranging exchange of ideas on social, cultural, economic, and political issues, to stimulate scholarly and public interest about Japan and U.S.-Japan relations, and to strengthen ties between Harvard University and Japan and between Japan and the United States.</M04>

<M02>First established in 1973 as the Japan Institute and renamed in 1985 to commemorate the retirement of its founder, the Reischauer Institute (RIJS) has, since its inception, taken the lead to build Harvard’s academic infrastructure for the study of Japan, engaging faculty and students in the humanities, social sciences, and sciences across the University, from the Faculty of Arts and Sciences (FAS) and the Graduate School of Arts and Sciences (GSAS), to the University’s professional schools.</M02>

<M04>Through its wide array of programs and initiatives, the Institute advances research in Japanese Studies, supports Harvard’s educational mission, and maintains ties with the Japanese Studies community throughout the U.S. and with individuals and institutions in Japan, Europe, and across the globe.</M04>

<M04>Professorships</M04>

<M04>The Institute endows six professorships within FAS in Anthropology, Cultural History, Japanese Politics, Japanese Religions, Japanese Religions and Society, and Sociology and provides for two junior professorships in East Asian Languages and Civilizations and Government.</M04>
<M04>Since fall 1982, RIJS has sponsored the Edwin O.</M04>
<M10>Reischauer Professorship in Japanese Studies, allowing senior faculty to devote up to an academic year to full-time research during their time at Harvard and, in alternate years, allowing the appointment of a leading scholar in Japanese Studies as a visiting professor.</M10>

<OTHER>Faculty</OTHER>

<M04>The Institute’s mission is carried out by a 50-member Full Committee composed of tenured professors, junior faculty, emeritus faculty, Japanese language faculty, and librarians from across the University whose research and teaching relate to Japan.</M04>
<M10>A smaller Executive Committee (EC) of 17 tenured faculty forms the governing body of the Institute.</M10>
<M04>Across all schools within the University, RIJS supports faculty research and teaching related to Japan, awarding grants for faculty projects, and funding course-related events and other departmental activities.</M04>

<M01>Postdoctoral Fellows</M01>

<M02>Initiated in 1980, the RIJS Postdoctoral Fellowship Program provides funding to 4-5 awardees in Japanese Studies each year.</M02>
<M02>The program is designed for those who have received their doctorate within the past five years.</M02>
<M04>Most devote their year at Harvard to producing publishable work from their dissertations, and they present their research in the Japan Forum series.</M04>
<M02>Since 2011, at least one postdoctoral fellow each year has been engaged in digital scholarship, and in 2018-19 RIJS established the Digital Fellowship/Digital Postdoctoral Fellowship in addition to its regular number of awards.</M02>

<OTHER>Visiting Scholars</OTHER>

<M02>Since 1973, RIJS has welcomed scholars from other universities and institutions in Japan and other parts of Asia, North America, Europe, and across the world.</M02>
<M06>These scholars typically spend one year at Harvard doing research, hosted by a Harvard faculty member.</M06>
<M04>They enrich the Harvard community by attending classes, pairing with students, participating in seminars, and offering students advice and assistance on their research as well as providing contacts for students’ future research in Japan.</M04>

<OTHER>Publications</OTHER>

<M07>The Institute contributes to Harvard’s print resources through subventions for Japan Studies monographs published by the Harvard University Asia Center and distributed by Harvard University Press through the Harvard East Asian Monograph (HEAM) series.</M07>
<M07>To date, more than 150 monographs on Japan have been published with RIJS support.</M07>
<M07>The works deal with all aspects of Japan, with particular emphases on Japan’s history, culture, society, and literature.</M07>
<M07>Other works, particularly in the humanities, are published in the Harvard-Yenching Institute Monograph Series, funded by the Harvard-Yenching Institute.</M07>
<M06>Works in both series are selected by the Asia Center Publications Committee, made up of Harvard faculty members, on the basis of peer reviews.</M06>

<OTHER>Libraries</OTHER>

<M06>The Institute is also able to contribute to Harvard’s print resources with the support of the Harvard-Yenching Library, which holds one of the largest collections of Japanese books outside Japan.</M06>
<M02>A print and digital library, the Documentation Center on Contemporary Japan (DCJ), was established by the Institute in 1988 and served for almost three decades as an important resource for scholars and students of contemporary Japan in the social sciences.</M02>
<M02>In 2017, the DCJ was transformed into the Japan Digital Research Center (JDRC) to reflect the recent shift toward online media and to expand its electronic resources on Japan.</M02>
<M06>This new center seeks to build new digital collections; develop services in support of research, teaching, and learning; and initiate various digital projects related to Japanese Studies, in collaboration with faculty and library staff across the University.</M06>

<M06>Digital Initiatives</M06>

<M02>To date, RIJS has established and maintains two major digital initiatives designed in response to current events, which gather and organize data that exist only online, so-called “born digital.”</M02>
<M02>The Constitutional Revision in Japan Research Project (CRRP) was created in 2005 at the inception of Japan’s reexamination of its postwar constitution.</M02>
<M02>The Japan Disasters Digital Archive Project (JDA) was formed in the aftermath of the Great East Japan Earthquake in March 2011.</M02>
<M04>Both projects are housed in the Japan Digital Research Center.</M04>
<M04>These web-based projects have enriched the Institute’s research activities and offerings while creating new networks among scholars and members of the public across the globe.</M04>

<OTHER>Courses</OTHER>

<M04>RIJS has increased Japan-related offerings in the curriculum in a number of ways.</M04>
<M04>Primary has been the establishment of six tenured professorships and two junior professorships.</M04>
<M02>In addition, from 1998-2010 the Institute’s Curriculum Enrichment Grant Program supported the creation of 13 new Core/General Education courses on Japan and augmentation of 7 existing courses taught by FAS faculty who were not Japan specialists but brought the study of Japan into their courses.</M02>
<M02>Typically there are more than 60 courses a year offered at Harvard with Japan-related content, in General Education, Anthropology, East Asian Languages and Civilizations, Government, History, History of Art and Architecture, History of Science, Religion, Sociology, Social Studies, and Visual and Environmental Studies within the Faculty of Arts and Sciences, as well as at the Harvard Summer School (HSS/Kyoto and others), Harvard Law School, Graduate School of Design, and Harvard Extension School.</M02>

<M02>Additional Support for the Curriculum</M02>

<M05>The Institute encourages faculty and departments to invite outstanding scholars or others with a specialized knowledge of Japan to appear in classes and meet with students and faculty in order to enhance the educational experience in their particular field.</M05>
<M10>RIJS also plays an active role in sponsoring activities outside the classroom that link art forms associated with Japanese popular culture, such as film, kabuki, martial arts, anime, and many others, to the educational curriculum.</M10>

<OTHER>Programs for Harvard Undergraduates</OTHER>

<M05>Since 1980, RIJS has supported more than 1200 Harvard College students to travel to Japan for research, language study, intercultural exchange, classes, conferences, as well as summer internships at a number of institutions and business organizations in Japan.</M05>

<M02>In 2005, RIJS expanded its summer internship program to include a broader circle of Harvard undergraduates, including those pursuing science concentrations, by offering placements to those with little or no prior training in the Japanese language.</M02>
<M02>This initiative reflects the University’s mandate to provide every college student with a “significant international experience.”</M02>
<M02>The following year saw a four-fold increase in the number of undergraduates traveling to Japan.</M02>
<M04>The number of internships has continued at a high level with a concomitant growth in the number of host institutions, broadening the networks between Japan and Harvard.</M04>

<M02>Summer opportunities were further augmented in 2007 when RIJS collaborated with the Harvard Summer School to create Harvard Summer School/Japan, a credit-bearing program that offers social science and humanities courses at Doshisha University and science courses RIKEN Brain Science Institute (BSI) and RIKEN Center for Integrative Medical Sciences (IMS).</M02>

<M07>On campus, a variety of undergraduate student groups, such as the Japan-America Student Conference (JASC), Harvard Project for Asian and International Relations (HPAIR), Harvard-Radcliffe Kendo Club, and the Harvard Asia-Pacific Review receive Institute support, and each year RIJS conducts a competition for the Noma-Reischauer Essay Prize for the best student essay on a Japan-related topic.</M07>

<OTHER>Programs for Graduate Students</OTHER>

<M04>The Institute’s support for graduate student training began even before its formal establishment (when the Japan Institute resided within the then-named Fairbank Center for East Asian Research).</M04>
<M05>Since 1970, RIJS funds have provided more than 1500 individual grants for graduate student research, language study, tuition support, and professional development.</M05>
<M05>Summer grants support research and language study, and term-time grants support supplementary dissertation research and dissertation completion.</M05>
<M05>RIJS also provides for graduate student travel to professional conferences and supports Japan-related organizations and study groups on campus.</M05>

<M02>Extending the Institute’s reach beyond the University are public events, cultural endeavors, and lectures, notably the weekly Japan Forum, which has sponsored nearly 800 talks since its inauguration in 1974.</M02>
<M04>Conferences, workshops, lectures, films, and exhibitions (many of them co-sponsored with other Harvard centers and departments) enhance the study of Japan on campus and draw participation from the surrounding community, across the U.S., Japan, and Europe, and around the world.</M04>

<M04>RIJS maintains close relations with numerous institutions throughout greater Boston and New England, including the Consulate General of Japan in Boston, the Japan Society of Boston, the Museum of Fine Arts and Peabody Essex Museum, as well other universities and schools.</M04>
<M04>By offering informal Associate-in-Research status to scholars and experts in various disciplines and fields relating to Japan, RIJS continues to build a network of relationships with MIT, Tufts, BU, and many other institutions mainly in the Northeast region.</M04>

<M08>Fostering close ties overseas, RIJS has concluded formal exchange agreements for faculty and graduate students with a number of prominent Japanese universities and research institutions.</M08>
<M02>For instance, its affiliation with Waseda University includes, among other programs, Waseda’s 125th Anniversary Commemorative Junior Visiting Researcher from Harvard University position.</M02>
<M02>The Institute holds corporate membership with International House of Japan and has a long association with Kodansha International, Ltd. In addition, various one-time events and exhibits enrich international exchange between Harvard and Japan, as well as the University’s academic life.</M02>

<M08>Through its summer internship program for Harvard College students, RIJS is closely associated with a wide range of Japanese universities, institutions, and businesses.</M08>
<M08>By expanding this program to include students with little or no prior training in the Japanese language, RIJS has broadened its networks in Japan to include not only its longtime partners such as the Rotary Club of Okayama, International Christian University, and Showa Women’s University, but also newer organizations, for example, Jenerate Partners, Inc., Tokyo Institute of Technology, Internet Business Japan, Hikari Tsushin, and NEXT Legal Corporation.</M08>

<M08>RIJS has also cultivated relationships with organizations in Japan through its ongoing digital initiatives.</M08>
<M02>The Constitutional Revision Research Project in Japan (CRRP), which established a joint-research agreement with Keio University in 2015, now involves scholars from Sophia, Hokkaido, Yonsei, and Waseda Universities and the University of Tokyo, among others.</M02>
<M06>The Japan Disasters Digital Archive Project (JDA) is affiliated with digital and non-digital partners in Japan, including the 311 Memories project of the National Institute of Informatics, the 311 Comprehensive Archive Project, Asahi Shimbun AJW, Great East Japan Earthquake Archives Fukushima, Kahoku Shimpo Disaster Archive, Michinoku Shinrokuden at Tohoku University, Sendai Mediatheque, the Tokyo Foundation, Yahoo!</M06>
<M06>JAPAN, Hidenori Watanave (Great East Japan Earthquake Archive), the National Diet Library of Japan, and NHK (Japan Broadcasting Corporation).</M06>
<M04>As the Institute continues to pursue these research endeavors, RIJS looks forward to strengthening these ties and making new collaborative relationships in the future.</M04>

<M01>Edwin Oldfather Reischauer</M01>

<M02>Edwin Oldfather Reischauer (October 1910 – September 1990) was born and raised in Tokyo, the son of Presbyterian educational missionaries.</M02>
<M07>At sixteen, he left Japan for Oberlin College, later taking up graduate work at Harvard where he studied East Asian history, including a five-year world study tour to Paris, Tokyo, Kyoto, and Beijing.</M07>
<M02>He returned to Harvard with his wife Adrienne in 1938, received his Ph.D. in 1939, and taught in the Department of Far Eastern Languages until 1941, when the State Department and the Army recruited him to serve variously as a research analyst, organizer of Japanese language programs for the military, and translator of intercepted military intelligence.</M02>
<M02>After his return to Harvard in 1946, Reischauer guided the development of a new curriculum in East Asian studies and began his career as a prolific writer.</M02>
<M02>It was during this “golden age” of teaching (to use his phrase) that he began his collaboration with John K.</M02>
<M05>Fairbank to teach a course on East Asian Civilizations, nicknamed “Rice Paddies,” which is still taught today as part of the General Education curriculum.</M05>

<M02>An article written by Reischauer in 1960, analyzing current tensions between the U.S. and Japan, caught the attention of U.S. President John F.</M02>
<M02>Kennedy, who appointed Reischauer as ambassador to Japan (1961-1966).</M02>
<M08>Key to Reischauer’s ambassadorial outlook was the notion of “equal partnership” between Japan and America.</M08>
<M02>He and his second wife Haru Matsukata, a journalist from Tokyo whom he had married after Adrienne’s death in 1955, gave priority to their ties with ordinary Japanese citizens and were enthusiastically received.</M02>
<M08>Both professionally and personally, Haru was a supportive companion to her husband and a strong partner to him as ambassador and scholar.</M08>

<M05>Returning to Harvard in 1966 as a University Professor, Reischauer continued to teach “Rice Paddies” and, reflecting his growing interest in contemporary issues, developed a course on Japanese Government and Politics in the Government Department and participated in a History Department course on The United States and East Asia.</M05>
<M07>Reischauer wrote many books, including East Asia: Tradition and Transformation (1973), co-authored with John K.</M07>
<M07>Fairbank and Albert M.</M07>
<M07>Craig.</M07>
<M02>He was the prime mover in establishing and funding the Japan Institute, later renamed in his honor, and he served as its director from 1974 to 1981.</M02>
<M02>Up until his retirement in 1981, he continued to teach, write, and initiate a myriad of projects to enhance relations between the U.S. and Japan, including producing a series of lectures on Japanese history on videotape for the University.</M02>
<M02>He was instrumental in expanding not only Harvard’s curriculum but the field of East Asian studies as a whole, deepening American consciousness of Japan and the outside world.</M02>
<M02>All of these contributions continue today to guide the Institute that gratefully carries his name.</M02>

<OTHER>The WHIRL</OTHER>

<M04>What is the WHIRL?</M04>

<M04>The WHIRL is a parable of What Happens In Real Life.</M04>

<M04>Think of the two ends as two different, but equal possibilities in your life.</M04>

<M04>When the WHIRL is whirling you see only one mark, the mark on the end that you pressed to set it in motion.</M04>
<M04>Which is to say: the start defines the ensuing experience.</M04>

<M04>Stop the WHIRL, and whirl again, this time pressing the other end, and now you see only the other mark.</M04>
<M04>It feels like magic, but only because we forget: the actual is merely a glimpse of the possible.</M04>
<M04>What happens in real life is always merely part of what can happen.</M04>
<M04>There are always other possibilities, invisible in the current whirl of life, but already there, waiting to be revealed.</M04>

<M05>But this, of course, is itself just one possible interpretation…</M05>

<OTHER>How to Whirl the WHIRL</OTHER>

<M04>Set the WHIRL on a hard, smooth surface.</M04>
<M04>Place your index finger near one end of the tube, slightly below the exact center of the top.</M04>
<M04>Bring your finger down quickly to make the tube roll backward and spin.</M04>

<OTHER>Location</OTHER>

<OTHER>The Edwin O.</OTHER>
<M04>Reischauer Institute of Japanese Studies is located on the second floor of the Center for Government and International Studies (CGIS) South Building, which is easily accessible by public transportation, taxi, or personal car.</M04>
<M10>Visitors are advised, however, that parking is scarce in the surrounding area; public transportation is highly recommended.</M10>
<M10>The CGIS South Building is a 5- to 10-minute walk from Harvard Yard and Harvard Square on the Red Line.</M10>

<M10>There is metered parking all along Broadway, Cambridge, Kirkland, and Oxford Streets near the CGIS South Building.</M10>
<M10>Paid Harvard parking is sometimes available in the nearby Broadway, Everett, and Oxford Garages, but a special visitor parking permit must be purchased in advance to use these facilities.</M10>
<M10>For more information, please contact Harvard University Parking Services at (617) 495-3772 or parking@harvard.edu.</M10>

<OTHER>Department of Germanic Languages and Literatures</OTHER>

<M10>Discover Our Department</M10>

<M10>We offer both an undergraduate and graduate program.</M10>
<M05>Undergraduates have the option to pursue a concentration in German Studies, Scandinavian Studies, or a joint concentration in German and another field.</M05>
<M05>Graduate students complete a PhD program in Germanic Languages and Literatures.</M05>
<M04>While the focus is on literature and cultural studies, the department encompasses and encourages interdisciplinary work.</M04>
<M04>Many graduate students include the study of interdisciplinary fields such as critical theory and philosophy, comparative approaches to literature, the history of science, social and political thought, film and media studies, art history, performance studies, the digital humanities, and musicology in their coursework and dissertation, taking full advantage of the incomparably rich and unique collections held at the Harvard Libraries, Art Museums, and Film Archive.</M04>

<M05>We offer a wide variety of courses in German, Nordic languages, and English on topics of cultural and historical interest.</M05>
<M05>Important figures such as Marx, Freud, Nietzsche, and Kafka are the subject of regular lecture courses, as are such topics as the Vikings and the Nordic heroic period, Nazi film, German social thought, German history, and Nordic cinema.</M05>
<M05>Smaller, discussion-type courses cover the age of Goethe, the relationship between Germany and the European Union, America in the German mind, German music, German and Scandinavian drama, German cinema, the role of sexuality and gender in German literature and art, media studies, and much more.</M05>

<M04>In addition, we maintain a vibrant series of events that reach from invited lectures and workshops to theater productions, musical events, and informal social gatherings, including language tables, our weekly Kaffeestunde, and other gatherings organized by the Harvard College German Club.</M04>
<M05>The close-knit community of graduate students organizes various events, including invited presentations by graduate students from other programs in the US and writing workshops addressing dissertation chapters, conference presentations, and articles in preparation.</M05>
<M05>Twice a semester, faculty and students meet for Sherry Hour, in which all of us discuss short contemporary literary texts selected by the graduate students.</M05>

<M01>Scholar Spotlights</M01>

<M01>Catrina De Rivera</M01>
<M02>Catrina De Rivera is a sixth-year graduate student, who is currently completing her dissertation, "Abject Legacy: Guilt, Shame, and Transgenerational Trauma," under the direction of Professors Eric Rentschler, Nicole Suetterlin, and Fatima Naqvi (Yale University).</M02>
<M02>"Abject Legacy" investigates some of the major shifts in memory discourse since the turn of the century, focusing on authors and directors descended from perpetrators who experiment with new approaches towards their families' pasts.</M02>

<M01>Robert Roessler</M01>
<M04>Before coming to Harvard, Robert studied German Literature and History at the University of Vienna and was a research assistant at the Ludwig Boltzmann Institute for the History and Theory of Biography.</M04>
<M04>At Harvard, Robert continues to specialize in literature from the turn of the 20th century, while also working on questions of knowledge formation and transformation, as well as cultural theory.</M04>

<M01>Aleksandra Kudryashova</M01>
<M05>Aleks Kudryashova is a PhD student based in the Department of Germanic Languages and Literatures and the Department of Art, Film, and Visual Studies at Harvard University.</M05>
<M04>Before joining the program, Aleks completed a Research Master in Literary and Cultural Studies at the University of Groningen (The Netherlands) and worked as coordinator and public affairs manager at the German Historical Institute Moscow (DHIM).</M04>

<M01>Daniel Carranza</M01>
<M02>Daniel Carranza joined the department in the Fall of 2020 as a Fellow in Germanic Languages and Literatures.</M02>
<M02>Dr. Carranza, who received his Ph.D. from the University of Chicago, joined the department’s faculty in July 2022 as Assistant Professor of Germanic Languages and Literatures.</M02>

<OTHER>History</OTHER>

<M02>The Department of Germanic Languages and Literatures looks back on a long and proud history.</M02>
<M02>Since the beginning of formal instruction in German language and literature at Harvard College in 1825 and the establishment of the department in 1897, some pre-eminent Germanists have taught at the department and have developed fresh theoretical and methodological approaches in their research and teaching.</M02>
